Product Name Cortex The AI Coach for Your Digital Diet. Problem Statement We don't have a storage problem; we have a retrieval and attention problem. Modern knowledge workers are addicted to the dopamine hit of hitting \"Save,\" \"Bookmark,\" or \"Add to Watch Later\"—a psychological trap that gives a false sense of productivity. This behavior creates digital landfills: fragmented silos of high-value information trapped across Instagram, YouTube, and browser extensions. Because current tools are entirely passive, users experience overwhelming friction and cognitive guilt when trying to retrieve what they’ve saved. Keyword search fails when you can't remember what you called something, and standard bookmarking apps require too much manual organization. Users are hoarding out of FOMO, but starving for actual, applied knowledge. Target Users Cortex is built for high-volume consumers of visual and instructional content who need actionable takeaways, not just endless feeds: Visual Designers & Creators: UI/UX designers, video editors, and content creators who hoard thousands of aesthetic references, complex software tutorials, and B-roll ideas. Knowledge Workers: Professionals who bookmark deep-dive industry articles, reports, and thought-leadership essays but never find the uninterrupted time to read them. Lifelong Learners: Hobbyists and self-starters trying to master new skills (e.g., photography gear setups, coding) who get overwhelmed by 40-minute tutorials. Current Alternatives Passive Bookmarkers (Pocket, Raindrop.io): These act as read-it-later black holes. They require manual tagging, offer zero synthesis, and wait for the user to initiate retrieval. High-Friction Workspaces (Notion, Obsidian, Roam): Immensely powerful, but they require the user to act as their own database administrator. The setup and maintenance friction is too high for casual, on-the-go saving. Walled Gardens (IG Collections, YouTube Playlists): Highly fragmented and siloed. You cannot connect an idea from an Instagram Reel to a concept in a Medium article. Unique Value Proposition (UVP) Cortex shifts the paradigm from \"Save and Forget\" to \"Capture and Coach.\" It is the first knowledge manager that actually cares about your cognitive load. Instead of demanding you organize your links, Cortex operates autonomously in the background to make your saved content work for you: Zero-Friction Ingestion: Auto-extracts metadata, transcripts, and context without a single manual tag. Cognitive Pacing: Maps content delivery to your energy levels. Tell Cortex you are \"Brain Dead,\" and it surfaces light visual inspiration. Product Name Cortex The AI Coach for Your Digital Diet. Problem Statement We don't have a storage problem; we have a retrieval and attention problem. Modern knowledge workers are addicted to the dopamine hit of hitting \"Save,\" \"Bookmark,\" or \"Add to Watch Later\"—a psychological trap that gives a false sense of productivity. This behavior creates digital landfills: fragmented silos of high-value information trapped across Instagram, YouTube, and browser extensions. Because current tools are entirely passive, users experience overwhelming friction and cognitive guilt when trying to retrieve what they’ve saved. Keyword search fails when you can't remember what you called something, and standard bookmarking apps require too much manual organization. Users are hoarding out of FOMO, but starving for actual, applied knowledge. Target Users Cortex is built for high-volume consumers of visual and instructional content who need actionable takeaways, not just endless feeds: Visual Designers & Creators: UI/UX designers, video editors, and content creators who hoard thousands of aesthetic references, complex software tutorials, and B-roll ideas. Knowledge Workers: Professionals who bookmark deep-dive industry articles, reports, and thought-leadership essays but never find the uninterrupted time to read them. Lifelong Learners: Hobbyists and self-starters trying to master new skills (e.g., photography gear setups, coding) who get overwhelmed by 40-minute tutorials. Current Alternatives Passive Bookmarkers (Pocket, Raindrop.io): These act as read-it-later black holes. They require manual tagging, offer zero synthesis, and wait for the user to initiate retrieval. High-Friction Workspaces (Notion, Obsidian, Roam): Immensely powerful, but they require the user to act as their own database administrator. The setup and maintenance friction is too high for casual, on-the-go saving. Walled Gardens (IG Collections, YouTube Playlists): Highly fragmented and siloed. You cannot connect an idea from an Instagram Reel to a concept in a Medium article. Unique Value Proposition (UVP) Cortex shifts the paradigm from \"Save and Forget\" to \"Capture and Coach.\" It is the first knowledge manager that actually cares about your cognitive load. Instead of demanding you organize your links, Cortex operates autonomously in the background to make your saved content work for you: Zero-Friction Ingestion: Auto-extracts metadata, transcripts, and context without a single manual tag. Cognitive Pacing: Maps content delivery to your energy levels. Tell Cortex you are \"Brain Dead,\" and it surfaces light visual inspiration. ResponseBody: {"success":true,"data":{"rawText":"1. Product Vision Document: Cortex | Notion. Purpose: Define why this product should exist.. Purpose: Define why this product should exist. Product Name Cortex The AI Coach for Your Digital Diet. Problem Statement We don't have a storage problem; we have a retrieval and attention problem. Modern knowledge workers are addicted to the dopamine hit of hitting \"Save,\" \"Bookmark,\" or \"Add to Watch Later\"—a psychological trap that gives a false sense of productivity. This behavior creates digital landfills: fragmented silos of high-value information trapped across Instagram, YouTube, and browser extensions. Because current tools are entirely passive, users experience overwhelming friction and cognitive guilt when trying to retrieve what they’ve saved. Keyword search fails when you can't remember what you called something, and standard bookmarking apps require too much manual organization. Knowledge Workers: Professionals who bookmark deep-dive industry articles, reports, and thought-leadership essays but never find the uninterrupted time to read them. Lifelong Learners: Hobbyists and self-starters trying to master new skills (e.g., photography gear setups, coding) who get overwhelmed by 40-minute tutorials. Current Alternatives Passive Bookmarkers (Pocket, Raindrop.io): These act as read-it-later black holes. They require manual tagging, offer zero synthesis, and wait for the user to initiate retrieval. High-Friction Workspaces (Notion, Obsidian, Roam): Immensely powerful, but they require the user to act as their own database administrator. The setup and maintenance friction is too high for casual, on-the-go saving. Walled Gardens (IG Collections, YouTube Playlists): Highly fragmented and siloed. Cognitive Pacing: Maps content delivery to your energy levels. Tell Cortex you are \"Brain Dead,\" and it surfaces light visual inspiration. Documentation IndexFetch the complete documentation index at: /llms.txtUse this file to discover all available pages before exploring further.Skip to main contentMCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open-source standard for connecting AI applications to external systems. Using MCP, AI applications like Claude or ChatGPT can connect to data sources (e.g. local files, databases), tools (e.g. search engines, calculators) and workflows (e.g. specialized prompts)—enabling them to access key information and perform tasks. Think of MCP like a USB-C port for AI applications. Just as USB-C provides a standardized way to connect electronic devices, MCP provides a standardized way to connect AI applications to external systems. ​What can MCP enable? Agents can access your Google Calendar and Notion, acting as a more personalized AI assistant. Claude Code can generate an entire web app using a Figma design. Enterprise chatbots can connect to multiple databases across an organization, empowering users to analyze data using chat. AI models can create 3D designs on Blender and print them out using a 3D printer. ​Why does MCP matter? Depending on where you sit in the ecosystem, MCP can have a range of benefits. Developers: MCP reduces development time and complexity when building, or integrating with, an AI application or agent. AI applications or agents: MCP provides access to an ecosystem of data sources, tools and apps which will enhance capabilities and improve the end-user experience. End-users: MCP results in more capable AI applications or agents which can access your data and take actions on your behalf when necessary. ​Broad ecosystem support MCP is an open protocol supported across a wide range of clients and servers. AI assistants like Claude and ChatGPT, development tools like Visual Studio Code, Cursor, MCPJam, and many others all support MCP — making it easy to build once and integrate everywhere. ​Start Building Build serversCreate MCP servers to expose your data and toolsBuild clientsDevelop applications that connect to MCP serversBuild MCP AppsBuild interactive apps that run inside AI clients ​Learn more Understand conceptsLearn the core concepts and architecture of MCPWas this page helpful?YesNoArchitecture⌘IAssistantResponses are generated using AI and may contain mistakes.","quickSparkSummary":"**Model Context Protocol (MCP)** is an open-","actionItems":[{"id":"bdf5aeb0-58a4-424b-b0cb-bd5c5c6316b0","itemType":1,"description":"What is the Model Context Protocol (MCP)? Documentation IndexFetch the complete documentation index at: /llms.txtUse this file to discover all available pages before exploring further.Skip to main contentMCP (Model Context Protocol) is an open-source standard for connecting AI applications to external systems. Using MCP, AI applications like Claude or ChatGPT can connect to data sources (e.g. local files, databases), tools (e.g. search engines, calculators) and workflows (e.g. specialized prompts)—enabling them to access key information and perform tasks. Think of MCP like a USB-C port for AI applications. Just as USB-C provides a standardized way to connect electronic devices, MCP provides a standardized way to connect AI applications to external systems. ​What can MCP enable? Agents can access your Google Calendar and Notion, acting as a more personalized AI assistant. Claude Code can generate an entire web app using a Figma design. Enterprise chatbots can connect to multiple databases across an organization, empowering users to analyze data using chat. AI models can create 3D designs on Blender and print them out using a 3D printer. ​Why does MCP matter? Depending on where you sit in the ecosystem, MCP can have a range of benefits. Developers: MCP reduces development time and complexity when building, or integrating with, an AI application or agent. AI applications or agents: MCP provides access to an ecosystem of data sources, tools and apps which will enhance capabilities and improve the end-user experience. End-users: MCP results in more capable AI applications or agents which can access your data and take actions on your behalf when necessary. ​Broad ecosystem support MCP is an open protocol supported across a wide range of clients and servers. AI assistants like Claude and ChatGPT, development tools like Visual Studio Code, Cursor, MCPJam, and many others all support MCP — making it easy to build once and integrate everywhere. ​Start Building Build serversCreate MCP servers to expose your data and toolsBuild clientsDevelop applications that connect to MCP serversBuild MCP AppsBuild interactive apps that run inside AI clients ​Learn more Understand conceptsLearn the core concepts and architecture of MCPWas this page helpful?YesNoArchitecture⌘IAssistantResponses are generated using AI and may contain mistakes.","quickSparkSummary":"**Model Context Protocol (MCP)** is an open-","actionItems":[{"id":"bdf5aeb0-58a4-424b-b0cb-bd5c5c6316b0","itemType":1,"description":"What is the Model Context Protocol (MCP)?
